=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a string S of length N and a string T of length M, both consisting of digits from 1 to 9.
You will perform the following operation for k=1,2,\ldots,M in order:

- Choose an integer i such that 1 \le i \le N. Then, replace the i-th character of S with the k-th character of T.

Find the maximum possible value of the resulting string S interpreted as an integer after performing the M operations.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N M
S
T

Output

Print the maximum possible value of the resulting string S interpreted as an integer after performing the M operations.

Constraints


- 1 \le N,M \le 10^6
- N and M are integers.
- S is a string of length N consisting of digits from 1 through 9.
- T is a string of length M consisting of digits from 1 through 9.

Sample Input 1

3 3
191
325

Sample Output 1

593

The following sequence of operations is optimal:

- For k=1: Choose i=3. Then, S = 193.
- For k=2: Choose i=1. Then, S = 293.
- For k=3: Choose i=1. Then, S = 593.

In this case, the value of S interpreted as an integer is 593, which is the maximum.

Sample Input 2

3 9
191
998244353

Sample Output 2

993

Sample Input 3

11 13
31415926535
2718281828459

Sample Output 3

98888976555

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.


Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's formalize cases.

Case A (mandatory visible):
- We have counts cnt (total T). Budget B = N (max visible changes).
- Need at least one visible change to d.
- No restriction on original d positions.

For a state (i, cnt_rem, B_rem, d_used):
- If d_used: completion is always possible? We can always keep remaining positions; no need to use remaining budget/counts. So yes, any state with d_used True is feasible. Wait if we have already used d visibly, we can ignore remaining budget/counts and just keep suffix. So feasibility true. Thus in greedy, once d_used, we can just apply unconstrained greedy (using remaining counts/budget if beneficial) to maximize suffix. Actually for max suffix, we should still use beneficial digits, but feasibility is true.
- If d_used False: Need to use d visibly at some position in suffix. Conditions:
   * count_rem[d] > 0 (we have a d digit available to use)
   * B_rem > 0 (budget slot for that visible change)
   * There exists at least one position j >= i with S[j] != d (so changing it to d is visible). If all remaining S[j]=d, impossible.
Are these conditions sufficient? Suppose we also need to ensure that if we use other digits before d, we leave budget and count. But feasibility of state just asks existence of some completion, not necessarily greedy. We can always choose to keep all positions until the last suitable position and then change it to d. That uses one budget and one d. So yes, if conditions hold, there is a completion: keep everything until some j with S[j]!=d, change j to d, keep rest. We don't need use other digits. Thus feasibility is simple! Great. Because we can always ignore other counts. So for Case A, d_used false feasible iff cnt_rem[d]>0 and B_rem>0 and suffix_has_non_d[i]. Since cnt_rem[d] initially count[d] and we only decrement if we use d visibly (or if we use d as non-visible? We wouldn't use d unless visible; but if S[i]=d and we choose y=d, no count decrement because no visible change. If we choose y=d and S[i]!=d, d_used becomes true. So cnt_rem[d] remains count[d] until d_used. Actually if we use d visibly, d_used true, no need. If we use d as a non-d? no. So cnt_rem[d] is always initial count[d] until d_used. But if we choose y=d at S[i]!=d, we decrement count[d] and set d_used true. After that feasibility true regardless. So for d_used false, cnt_rem[d] = initial count[d] >0. Unless we somehow consumed d without d_used? Not possible. So condition reduces to B_rem>0 and suffix_has_non_d[i]. But wait if initial count[d] could be 0? T[M]=d so count[d]>=1. So yes.

But what if we use d visibly and also need count[d] for other visible changes? Once d_used true, we can use remaining d counts if beneficial. Feasibility true.

Thus Case A greedy is simple:
- Maintain budget B_rem = N.
- Maintain counts cnt_rem for all digits.
- Maintain d_used False.
- Precompute suffix_has_non_d[i]: whether there is any position j>=i with S[j] != d.
- For i from 0 to N-1:
   If d_used:
       Choose y = max digit x in 1..9 such that cnt_rem[x] >0 and x > S[i] and B_rem >0? Actually if we choose y > S[i], visible change, consume count and budget. If no such, keep y=S[i]. This is unconstrained greedy.
   Else:
       We need choose largest y such that after choice, either d_used becomes true, or d_used false and state feasible (B_rem_after >0 and suffix_has_non_d[i+1]).
       Candidates:
         - Keep y = S[i]. No count/budget. If S[i] != d? d_used remains false. Need B_rem>0 and suffix_has_non_d[i+1]. If S[i] == d, keeping original d does not satisfy visible; d_used remains false. Need same.
         - Change to y > S[i] (visible) using count[y]>0 and B_rem>0.
              If y == d: d_used becomes True. Then no further condition (feasible).
              If y != d: d_used remains False. Need B_rem-1 >0 and suffix_has_non_d[i+1] (since d must be used later). Also count_rem[d]>0 (true).
         - Change to y < S[i]? This would be a visible decrease. Could it ever be chosen in Case A before d_used? If y == d and d < S[i], using d now would satisfy mandatory but decrease current. Could be necessary if no later position !=d and B_rem>0? But if no later position !=d, then suffix_has_non_d[i+1]=False. To satisfy mandatory, we must use d now (if S[i]!=d). If d < S[i], we might be forced to decrease current. Greedy should consider y=d even if d < S[i] when no later non-d and d_used false. Also if d > S[i], it's included in y > S[i]. If d == S[i], no visible. So we need consider y=d as a visible change whenever S[i] != d and count[d]>0 and B_rem>0, regardless of y > S[i]? If d < S[i], it's a decrease but may be necessary. However, if d < S[i] and there is a later non-d, we would prefer to defer to later (rightmost) to minimize lexicographic loss. The greedy with feasibility will choose largest y. At current position, if there is any y > S[i] feasible, it will choose that. If not, it may choose keep if feasible. If keep feasible (i.e., can use d later), then choosing d < S[i] now would make current lower than S[i], so keep is larger and feasible, so greedy will choose keep. If keep not feasible (no later non-d), then it must choose d now (decrease) if S[i]!=d. So we need include y=d even if d < S[i] as a candidate when d_used false and S[i]!=d. What about y < S[i] but y != d? Never needed, would be a decrease and not satisfy mandatory; not beneficial. So only forced decrease is d.
         - Change to y = d when S[i] != d and count[d]>0 and B_rem>0: d_used true. This is always feasible (completion possible by keeping rest). Even if d < S[i], it's a candidate. But if keep feasible, keep > d, so not chosen. If a larger y > S[i] feasible, chosen.
       Also, what if y = d and d == S[i]? Not visible, doesn't set d_used. It's same as keep; no count consumed. So no special.
   After choosing y:
      If y != S[i]: B_rem -=1; cnt_rem[y]-=1; if y==d: d_used=True.
      Else no change. But if y==S[i] and S[i]==d, d_used remains false (visible mandatory not satisfied). Good.
Need ensure when d_used false and we choose y != d visible, we don't consume d count. cnt_rem[d] remains.

But wait: In Case A, if we choose y != d visible before d_used, we consume budget. Feasibility condition B_rem_after >0 ensures one budget left for d. But what if B_rem_after =0 and d_used false, infeasible. So we won't choose a non-d visible if budget is 1. Good.

However, is it always optimal to use non-d visible digits before d if it leaves budget for d? Suppose current S[i]=1, available digits 9 and d=8, budget=2. Greedy candidates:
- y=9 (non-d) feasible if B_after=1>0 and suffix_has_non_d. Choose 9. Later use d. Good.
Suppose current S[i]=1, available digits 9 and d=8, budget=1. Non-d 9 infeasible because no budget for d. Candidate y=8 (d) feasible, choose 8. Is that optimal? We must use d visibly; with budget 1, we can either use 9 now and fail mandatory, or use d now. So final current 8. Alternative use d later and keep current 1? But budget 1, if keep current, can use d later at some position. If there is a later position with S<8, final might be e.g., current 1, later 8. Compare using d now: current 8, later original. Lexicographically, current 8 > current 1, so using d now is better if d > S[i]. So greedy chooses 8. Good.
Suppose d=2, S[i]=9, budget=1, no later non-d. Greedy candidates: keep infeasible (no later non-d), y=d=2 <9 feasible, choose 2. Final decreases. Good.

So Case A greedy seems correct.

But we also need consider that after d_used true, we should continue using remaining counts/budget to maximize suffix. The simple unconstrained greedy (only replace if digit > S[i]) is optimal. But is it ever beneficial to use a digit <= S[i] after d_used? No, because we can choose not to select it; no mandatory. So no.

Now Case B (mandatory hidden):
- We do not visibly use d. Need at least one original d position remains unchanged.
- Budget B = N-1 visible changes (because mandatory hidden consumes one operation slot). But wait if N=0? N>=1. If N=1 and original has d, B=0; no visible changes. If original no d, Case B impossible.
- We can use up to B visible changes, none with value d (since if we change to d, it's Case A; Case B specifically no visible d). We can use other digits from T counts, but cannot use d visibly. However, we might use d as a visible change? No, that would be Case A. For Case B, C contains no d. So counts for d are unavailable for visible changes. But we can still use other counts.
- Need leave at least one original d position unchanged. This means we cannot change all positions where S[i]=d. Since we only change positions to non-d digits in Case B, any changed original d position becomes non-d and no longer provides d. We need at least one original d not changed. If there is no original d, Case B impossible.

Feasibility for a state in Case B:
State: position i, cnt_rem (excluding d? we won't use d), B_rem, and a flag d_preserved? Actually we need ensure at least one original d remains unchanged in the suffix or already preserved in prefix. Since we process left to right, we can track whether we have already "preserved" an original d by keeping it unchanged. But if we keep an original d at position i, then mandatory can be hidden there; we don't need to preserve another later. However, if we later change that position? We won't, because we process left to right and final digit fixed. So once we keep an original d, preserved=True. If we change an original d to non-d, it is not preserved. We need final preserved True.

But for feasibility of a state, we can always choose to keep some future original d if any remains. So condition: if not preserved yet, there must exist at least one position j>=i with S[j]=d that we will keep. Since we can choose to keep it, feasibility requires suffix_has_d[i] (original d in suffix) and B_rem? Actually keeping doesn't consume budget. So if suffix_has_d[i], we can preserve one. But if we change all original d before preserving, infeasible. In greedy, we can decide.

Also, we have budget B_rem for visible changes (non-d). We don't have to use all. Feasibility of state is always true if we can keep rest, as long as if preserved flag false, suffix_has_d[i] true. Because we can ignore counts/budget and keep all remaining positions, preserving a d. So Case B feasibility is simple: if not preserved, need some original d in suffix; if preserved, always true. Counts/budget don't matter for feasibility because we can choose not to use them. Wait but if we have already made some visible changes, they are fixed; can we always complete by keeping rest? Yes, no mandatory visible. So any state is feasible provided we can still satisfy hidden preservation: either preserved already or there is an original d in remaining suffix. Counts/budget irrelevant for feasibility. This means greedy for Case B is straightforward:
- We have budget B = N-1.
- Counts cnt_rem for digits 1..9, but digit d cannot be used visibly. We can use other digits.
- Track preserved = False (whether we have kept an original d).
- Precompute suffix_has_d[i] (original S[j]==d for j>=i).
- For i from 0 to N-1:
   We want choose largest y such that after choice, if preserved not yet, there remains an original d in suffix (or we preserve now).
   Candidates:
     - Keep y = S[i]. No budget/count. If S[i]==d, preserved becomes True. If S[i]!=d, preserved unchanged. Feasibility after keep: if not preserved, need suffix_has_d[i+1] (since if current is not d, no preserve; if current is d, preserved true). If preserved true, feasible.
     - Change to y > S[i] using count[y]>0 and y != d? Actually if y == d and S[i]!=d, that would be visible d, not allowed in Case B. So y cannot be d if visible. If S[i]==d and y==d, no visible change (keep). So visible changes use y != d. Also y could be < S[i]? Never beneficial and not needed; no mandatory visible. So only consider y > S[i], y != d, count[y]>0, B_rem>0.
       After change, if S[i]==d, then this original d is lost; preserved remains unchanged (if it was false, still false; if true, still true). If S[i]!=d, preserved unchanged. Need if not preserved, suffix_has_d[i+1] true (there must be another original d later to preserve). If preserved true, feasible.
     - What about changing to y < S[i] but y != d? Never beneficial, ignore.
   Choose largest y among keep and beneficial changes that are feasible.
- At the end, need preserved True. If not, Case B invalid. But greedy should ensure if possible; if no feasible candidate at some position, Case B impossible. Actually if at last position and not preserved and S[i]!=d, keep infeasible (no suffix d), changes infeasible (no future d), so no candidate -> Case B impossible. If S[i]==d, keep preserves -> feasible.

Is this greedy optimal for Case B? Let's test. Since feasibility only requires preserving a d, and we can always keep rest, the only reason to avoid changing an original d is if it's the last one. Greedy with feasibility will allow changing original d if there is another d later. This seems right. It will use beneficial non-d digits greedily. But is there any interaction with counts/budget and future beneficial digits? Greedy using max available digit > S[i] is optimal as argued. Feasibility check ensures we don't destroy last d. Could using the largest x now make it impossible to use some other digit later, but current dominates; any completion with current x is lexicographically larger than any completion with current < x. So yes. This is a standard greedy.

Now, let's examine the original operation order and selected subset reduction more rigorously. We should be certain that any final string satisfying our constraints is feasible with the original sequential operations. We argued selected subset including M assigned to positions; all unselected operations dumped to M position. But what about selected operations that are not assigned in chronological order? We assign each selected operation index to a distinct position. For a position assigned selected time l, we need ensure no later operation (selected or unselected) is assigned to that position. We can arrange:
- For each selected operation k, at time k, write T[k] to its assigned position.
- For each unselected operation k, at time k, write T[k] to the position assigned to M (the last operation). Since M is selected and assigned to some position p_M. For any unselected k < M, writing to p_M is overwritten at time M by T[M]. For selected operations assigned to other positions, no unselected operation writes to them. But what about selected operations with time > k? They write to their own positions, not p_M. So p_M only receives unselected operations and M. Good.
- What about selected operation M? At time M, write to p_M. No later operations. Good.
- What about selected operation k that is assigned to a position p, and there are unselected operations after k that are dumped to p_M, not p. So p remains T[k]. Good.
Thus any subset of operation indices including M, assigned to distinct positions, is feasible. We can choose any subset of T digits with counts; if we select multiple operations with same digit, distinct indices. Since counts from T, possible. Good.

But wait: In d_used false branch, we might choose a non-d visible change x > sv. We decrement cntA[x]. What if x is d? We skip non-d loop; d candidate handles. Good.

Now, Case B function:
def case_B(cnt, N, S_b, d_ascii, suffix_d):
    cntB = cnt[:]
    B = N - 1
    preserved = False
    res = bytearray(N)
    for i in range(N):
        s = S_b[i]
        sv = s - 48
        best_ascii = -1
        # Candidate keep:
        # If preserved already, keep always feasible.
        # If not preserved:
        #   if s == d_ascii, keeping preserves -> feasible.
        #   else need suffix_d[i+1] (some later original d)
        keep_feasible = preserved or (s == d_ascii) or suffix_d[i+1]
        if keep_feasible:
            best_ascii = s
        # Candidate visible change to x > sv, x != d, cntB[x]>0, B>0
        # Feasibility after change:
        #   if not preserved and s == d_ascii: need suffix_d[i+1] (another d later)
        #   if not preserved and s != d_ascii: need suffix_d[i+1] (some d later)
        #   if preserved: feasible
        # So if not preserved, need suffix_d[i+1] regardless? If s==d and we change it, need later d. If s!=d, need later d to preserve. So yes, if not preserved, need suffix_d[i+1]. If preserved, no need.
        if B > 0:
            change_feasible_base = preserved or suffix_d[i+1]
            if change_feasible_base:
                # We can change to any x > sv, x != d with cntB[x]>0.
                # Since current digit would be x, choose largest x > sv.
                for x in range(9, sv, -1):
                    if x == d:
                        continue
                    if cntB[x] > 0:
                        cand = x + 48
                        if cand > best_ascii:
                            best_ascii = cand
                        break  # largest x > sv non-d
        if best_ascii == -1:
            return None
        chosen = best_ascii
        res[i] = chosen
        if chosen != s:
            B -= 1
            x = chosen - 48
            cntB[x] -= 1
            # if s == d_ascii, this original d is lost; preserved remains as before
            # if chosen == d? We disallowed, so no.
        else:
            # keep
            if s == d_ascii:
                preserved = True
        # If we changed a non-d position, preserved unchanged.
    if not preserved:
        return None
    return res

Now, let's examine the implementation of Case A candidate selection more carefully to avoid bugs with best_ascii and keep.

Pseudo:

def case_A(cnt, N, S_b, d_ascii, suffix_non):
    cnt = cnt[:]
    B = N
    d_used = False
    res = bytearray(N)
    d = d_ascii - 48
    for i in range(N):
        s = S_b[i]
        sv = s - 48
        if d_used:
            chosen = s
            if B > 0:
                for x in range(9, sv, -1):
                    if cnt[x] > 0:
                        chosen = x + 48
                        cnt[x] -= 1
                        B -= 1
                        break
            res[i] = chosen
        else:
            best = -1
            # keep
            if B > 0 and suffix_non[i+1]:
                best = s
            # visible d
            if B > 0 and s != d_ascii and cnt[d] > 0:
                cand = d_ascii
                if cand > best:
                    best = cand
            # non-d beneficial changes x > sv, x != d
            if B > 1 and suffix_non[i+1]:
                for x in range(9, sv, -1):
                    if x == d:
                        continue
                    if cnt[x] > 0:
                        cand = x + 48
                        if cand > best:
                            best = cand
                        break  # since x descending, this is largest non-d > sv; but wait if d > x and d candidate was considered, best may already be d. If d < x, this cand > d. If d > x, d candidate > cand. So break okay.
            if best == -1:
                return None
            chosen = best
            res[i] = chosen
            if chosen != s:
                B -= 1
                x = chosen - 48
                cnt[x] -= 1
                if x == d:
                    d_used = True
    if not d_used:
        return None
    return res

But what if d candidate 9 was infeasible? d candidate is feasible if s != d_ascii and cnt[9]>0 and B>0. If cnt[9]>0, best=9. If cnt[9]==0, non-d loop skips 9 and finds8. Good. If d=5, sv=1, non-d loop x from9 down to2, skips5, finds maybe9. d candidate 5 considered. If cnt9>0, best9. If cnt9==0, finds8 etc. If no non-d >sv except d, loop may skip d and find none, but d candidate best set. Good.
But what if d > sv and d candidate is feasible, and there is a non-d x > d feasible? The non-d loop will find x > d before skipping d? Since loop descending from9, if x=9 >d and cnt9>0, it will choose9, break, best=9. If x=8 >d, choose8. If no x>d, it will eventually reach d and skip, but d candidate best already. Good.
What if d < sv (decrease), d candidate considered, non-d loop only x > sv, so no non-d. If keep infeasible, best=d. Good.
What if d == sv (s==d_ascii), d candidate skipped, non-d loop x > sv. If keep feasible and no non-d, best=s. If keep infeasible and no non-d, best=-1 -> Case A impossible. This matches: if current is original d and no later non-d, visible d cannot be used now or later. But what if we could change current original d to a non-d x > d and later use d at a later non-d? If suffix_non[i+1] false, non-d loop not run because B>1 and suffix_non false; but could we change current d to non-d and then have no later non-d, so no visible d; impossible. Good.

Now, Case B implementation with break in non-d loop:

Now, let's maybe derive a more direct proof of Case A greedy to ensure no hidden flaw with "keep" feasibility when d_used false and B>0 but suffix_non[i+1] true. Suppose we choose keep at current, but later we might want to change a position to d that is before some other beneficial changes. Feasibility only needs existence. The greedy will later choose optimally among feasible choices. Since the current choice is feasible, there is at least one complete final string. The greedy's later choices maintain feasibility. So by induction, it constructs a feasible final string. It chooses the largest possible current digit among all feasible choices. Therefore lexicographically optimal. This is standard. We don't need to specify the completion.

Let's write code with local variables for speed:
def case_a(cnt, N, S_b, d_ascii, suffix_non):
    cnt = cnt[:]
    B = N
    d_used = False
    res = bytearray(N)
    d = d_ascii - 48
    for i, s in enumerate(S_b):
        sv = s - 48
        if d_used:
            chosen = s
            if B:
                for x in range(9, sv, -1):
                    if cnt[x]:
                        chosen = x + 48
                        cnt[x] -= 1
                        B -= 1
                        break
            res[i] = chosen
        else:
            best = -1
            if B and suffix_non[i+1]:
                best = s
            if B and s != d_ascii and cnt[d]:
                if d_ascii > best:
                    best = d_ascii
            if B > 1 and suffix_non[i+1]:
                for x in range(9, sv, -1):
                    if x == d:
                        continue
                    if cnt[x]:
                        cand = x + 48
                        if cand > best:
                            best = cand
                        break
            if best < 0:
                return None
            chosen = best
            res[i] = chosen
            if chosen != s:
                B -= 1
                x = chosen - 48
                cnt[x] -= 1
                if x == d:
                    d_used = True
    if not d_used:
        return None
    return res

Wait, in d_used false branch, the non-d loop break after first feasible x. But what if the first feasible x is not > best because best is keep s and x > sv so x > s, so cand > best. If best is d_ascii and x > d_ascii, cand > best; if x < d_ascii, then d_ascii would have been found earlier? Let's see: We consider d candidate before non-d loop. If d > sv, d_ascii is a candidate. Non-d loop starts from 9 down. If there is a non-d x > d, it will be found before reaching d (skipped) and cand > d. If no non-d x > d, the loop will eventually skip d and then find maybe x < d. But if d candidate exists, best=d. The loop will continue after skipping d? Wait our code breaks on first feasible non-d x. If it encounters x > d and cnt[x]>0, it breaks with cand > best. If it encounters x = d, it skips. If it then encounters x < d with cnt[x]>0, it breaks with cand = x+48 < d_ascii. But best is already d_ascii, so cand > best is false, so best remains d. Then break. This is okay because no larger non-d than d exists below d. But what if d candidate was not feasible (cnt[d]==0 or s==d_ascii)? Then best might be keep s. If s==d_ascii, then sv=d, non-d loop starts from 9 down to d+1, so it won't go below d because range(9, sv, -1) stops at sv+1. So no x<d considered. If cnt[d]==0 (impossible) and s!=d, best maybe keep; non-d loop might find x<d and cand > keep? If x > sv, then x > s, so cand > keep, so update. Good. If best is -1 and non-d loop finds x<d, cand > -1, update. So break okay.

But wait, in non-d loop, if d > sv and cnt[d]>0, d candidate considered. Suppose there is a non-d x > d but cnt[x]==0, then loop reaches d and skips, then finds x < d. Now, Case B code:

def case_b(cnt, N, S_b, d_ascii, suffix_d):
    cnt = cnt[:]
    B = N - 1
    preserved = False
    res = bytearray(N)
    d = d_ascii - 48
    for i, s in enumerate(S_b):
        sv = s - 48
        best = -1
        if preserved or s == d_ascii or suffix_d[i+1]:
            best = s
        if B:
            if preserved or suffix_d[i+1]:
                for x in range(9, sv, -1):
                    if x == d:
                        continue
                    if cnt[x]:
                        cand = x + 48
                        if cand > best:
                            best = cand
                        break
        if best < 0:
            return None
        chosen = best
        res[i] = chosen
        if chosen != s:
            B -= 1
            x = chosen - 48
            cnt[x] -= 1
        else:
            if s == d_ascii:
                preserved = True
    if not preserved:
        return None
    return res

Need check keep_feasible condition: `preserved or s == d_ascii or suffix_d[i+1]`. If preserved false, s!=d, suffix_d[i+1] true -> keep feasible. If preserved false, s==d -> keep feasible (preserve now). If preserved true -> keep feasible. Good.
Change_feasible: `preserved or suffix_d[i+1]`. If preserved false and s==d, need suffix_d[i+1] true to change this d and preserve later. If preserved false and s!=d, need suffix_d[i+1] true. If preserved true, can change any. Good.
But what if B>0 and change_feasible true, but the chosen x > sv is d? skip. Good.

</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys


def solve_case_visible(cnt, N, S, d_ascii, suffix_non):
    # Case A: the last operation is visible, i.e. some position is changed to d.
    cnt = cnt[:]
    B = N
    d_used = False
    d = d_ascii - 48
    res = bytearray(N)

    for i, s in enumerate(S):
        sv = s - 48

        if d_used:
            chosen = s
            if B:
                for x in range(9, sv, -1):
                    if cnt[x]:
                        chosen = x + 48
                        cnt[x] -= 1
                        B -= 1
                        break
            res[i] = chosen
            continue

        best = -1

        # Keep current digit, if d can still be used later.
        if B and suffix_non[i + 1]:
            best = s

        # Use d visibly now.
        if B and s != d_ascii and cnt[d]:
            if d_ascii > best:
                best = d_ascii

        # Use another digit > current, while still leaving a place/budget for d.
        if B > 1 and suffix_non[i + 1]:
            for x in range(9, sv, -1):
                if x == d:
                    continue
                if cnt[x]:
                    cand = x + 48
                    if cand > best:
                        best = cand
                    break

        if best < 0:
            return None

        chosen = best
        res[i] = chosen

        if chosen != s:
            B -= 1
            x = chosen - 48
            cnt[x] -= 1
            if x == d:
                d_used = True

    if not d_used:
        return None
    return res


def solve_case_hidden(cnt, N, S, d_ascii, suffix_d):
    # Case B: the last operation is hidden at an original d position.
    cnt = cnt[:]
    B = N - 1
    preserved = False
    d = d_ascii - 48
    res = bytearray(N)

    for i, s in enumerate(S):
        sv = s - 48
        best = -1

        # Keep current digit if we can still preserve an original d.
        if preserved or s == d_ascii or suffix_d[i + 1]:
            best = s

        # Change to a larger non-d digit if possible.
        if B and (preserved or suffix_d[i + 1]):
            for x in range(9, sv, -1):
                if x == d:
                    continue
                if cnt[x]:
                    cand = x + 48
                    if cand > best:
                        best = cand
                    break

        if best < 0:
            return None

        chosen = best
        res[i] = chosen

        if chosen != s:
            B -= 1
            x = chosen - 48
            cnt[x] -= 1
        else:
            if s == d_ascii:
                preserved = True

    if not preserved:
        return None
    return res


def main():
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
    N = int(data[0])
    M = int(data[1])
    S = data[2]
    T = data[3]

    d_ascii = T[-1]
    d = d_ascii - 48

    cnt = [0] * 10
    for ch in T:
        cnt[ch - 48] += 1

    suffix_non = bytearray(N + 1)
    suffix_d = bytearray(N + 1)
    has_non = 0
    has_d = 0

    for i in range(N - 1, -1, -1):
        if S[i] == d_ascii:
            has_d = 1
        else:
            has_non = 1
        suffix_non[i] = has_non
        suffix_d[i] = has_d

    ans_visible = solve_case_visible(cnt, N, S, d_ascii, suffix_non)
    ans_hidden = solve_case_hidden(cnt, N, S, d_ascii, suffix_d)

    if ans_visible is None:
        ans = ans_hidden
    elif ans_hidden is None:
        ans = ans_visible
    else:
        ans = ans_visible if ans_visible > ans_hidden else ans_hidden

    sys.stdout.write(ans.decode() + "\n")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
